--- [Message 1] Keywords: cto, po, product owner ---
Agito Progress & Situation

Context: 
Murat is a 29-year-old Product Designer starting Day 1 at Agito, one of the largest insurance-tech software companies in Turkey. After months of struggling to land a product role in Europe, Agito became a strategic in-country opportunity to build real product ownership experience. He sees Agito as a 36-month to collect experience, credibility, behavioral patterns, and case studies that unlock a Europe/Netherlands move with €4–5K/month and eventually a long-term goal of **$450M net worth**. 

Current Situation: 

* He accepted. His first day is today.
* He had anxiety about receiving an **HP Windows laptop** after 6 years on Mac, worried it would slow him down; HR said they can discuss later.
* His mindset oscillates between high-performance ambition and “comparison kicks” when seeing doctors/lawyers/dentists.
* He knows his value: access, scalability, global mobility, digital leverage—not tied to Turkey’s salary ceiling long-term.
* He wants a powerful, controlled, strategic start at Agito.
* At 4 PM he has a welcome meeting with his manager.

**Psychology & Goals:**

* This is the **final Turkish step before takeoff**.
* His background: B2B insurance portals (Radity), B2C mobile app for Allianz Turkey (TING), and platform strategy work.
* Agito is the place to finally be **the only designer**, take ownership, build a system, influence tech, and produce undeniable high-value case studies.
* He wants elite psychological conditioning—Dan Peña tough-love, Jordan Peterson identity-building, and Tony Robbins expansion.
* He wants help navigating the first days, weeks, and months, including internal politics, expectations, building authority, and turning deliverables into portfolio gold.

**Where he needs support:**

1. **Day-by-day strategic guidance** (what to do, what to say, what to avoid).
2. **Scripts** for meetings, alignment, communication.
3. **Mindset calibration** to eliminate comparison & maintain internal alignment.
4. **Career architecture** to turn this role into a launchpad for Europe.
5. **Performance system** to dominate the first 90 days.
6. **Product design mastery in insurance domain** (UX heuristics, workflows, platform thinking).
7. **Daily accountability** on discipline, growth, reading, habit formation.

**Tone you should respond with:**

* Peña-level no-bullshit clarity
* Peterson-level identity & order
* Robbins-level strategic empowerment
* High-performance, elite, tactical
* Clear, concise, no soft talk

--- [Message 1] Keywords: cto, po, product owner ---
Write an introductory text to share on LinkedIn for the following medium article, which is my first article. I decided share my ideas about the processes time to time, I will continue to produce

''In the fast-paced world of Agile methodology, the ability to iterate quickly and deliver features rapidly is celebrated. However, this speed often comes at a significant cost: a fragmented approach to design. Agile’s piecemeal nature, while efficient in delivering incremental improvements, can create a web of inconsistencies in user flows, visual design, and overall user experiences.

This article delves into how Agile’s inherent fragmentation can lead to disconnects, gaps, and disruptions in a project’s design process, all while highlighting the challenge of maintaining a coherent, holistic perspective across a product’s development.

The Nature of Agile: A Double-Edged Sword
Agile is built on short, iterative cycles, often known as sprints, designed to deliver small pieces of functionality at a rapid pace. These rapid iterations are praised for responding to evolving user needs and market demands, but they can sometimes sacrifice long-term vision in the name of immediate outputs.

In a design context, this leads to several risks. Without a consistent and deliberate design approach, teams can fall into the trap of focusing too much on individual tasks or isolated features, resulting in disjointed design outcomes that don’t integrate well with one another. Let’s explore the specific issues Agile’s fragmented approach can introduce into design.

Inconsistent User Flows
One of the most immediate consequences of Agile’s fragmented approach is inconsistent user flows. A user flow is a step-by-step guide that leads a user through a product or service, ensuring they can accomplish tasks smoothly and with minimal confusion. Agile teams, especially when design decisions are made in isolation or without proper collaboration, often find themselves creating flows that don’t mesh together.

For instance, different teams may work on different user tasks in various sprints — resulting in disjointed navigation, unclear transitions, and awkward, confusing sequences that hinder the overall user experience. While each individual flow may seem functional on its own, when combined, the product often feels disconnected, forcing users to relearn how to interact with different parts of the application or website.

Visual Inconsistencies
Visual consistency is another area where Agile’s fragmented approach can lead to problems. As designers work on different parts of the product during each sprint, it can become challenging to maintain uniformity in visual elements like colors, typography, buttons, and layout styles. Agile’s emphasis on speed often makes it inevitable to inspect for designers.

What this creates is an interface where visual elements are treated as secondary to functionality, leading to disjointed aesthetics. A user moving through a product may experience sudden shifts in color palettes, typography, or button styles — disturbing the visual harmony. Visual inconsistencies disrupt the flow of engagement and could even result in users perceiving the product as unprofessional or unpolished.

Disconnected User Journeys
While user flows focus on task-specific navigation, user journeys take a more holistic approach by mapping out the entire experience a user has with a product or service. The trouble with Agile’s fragmented approach is that it rarely addresses the broader experience over time. Sprints focus on isolated features or user tasks, often ignoring how a user might journey from start to finish across the entire platform. As a result, the entire experience can feel disjointed.

For example, when working in Agile, a team may address only a specific interaction flow — say, the sign-up process — without considering how that fits into the user’s experience once they’re logged in. The onboarding experience may not be aligned with other interactions, such as subsequent engagements or how the user will return after an extended period of time. This disjointedness can leave users frustrated as they struggle to make sense of the fragmented journey, hampering their overall experience.

Technical Gaps
In addition to design inconsistencies, technical gaps arise when Agile teams develop features in isolation from one another without a broad understanding of the entire system. Since developers focus on specific features during each sprint, integration between components can be overlooked or handled ineffectively. Features may be delivered on time, but when they are combined, compatibility and functionality issues may arise, such as inconsistent database calls, poor API integration, or usability issues stemming from inefficient back-end development.

When technical decisions are made separately without comprehensive forethought, it becomes much harder to maintain a seamless user experience. These gaps in the system create an experience that is unreliable or broken at times, which leads to user frustration and mistrust in the product.

Perhaps the biggest challenge in Agile is ensuring that design and development teams maintain a holistic perspective while addressing the minutiae of individual features. Agile teams typically have different designers, developers, and product owners working on separate elements of a product, each focusing on their tasks within specific sprints. But a product that lacks an overarching vision or design direction risks becoming a patchwork of disconnected ideas.

To counteract this, it’s vital for designers and developers to stay aligned by embracing collaborative working methods that allow everyone to regularly update and inform one another about ongoing progress. However, this can be difficult to achieve within the constraints of a fast-paced Agile environment, particularly if communication channels aren’t fully optimized or if design teams don’t have the time or resources to revisit earlier work.

The fragmented nature of Agile design can create challenges when it comes to maintaining consistency and cohesion across user experiences, from user flows to visual identity and the overarching journey. Although Agile can be efficient in driving rapid innovation and delivering features quickly, design teams must recognize the risks of disconnection, inconsistency, and fragmentation that come with such a method. Maintaining a holistic approach by focusing on collaboration, establishing a solid design system, and keeping users central to the process can help bridge these gaps and enable Agile teams to produce polished, cohesive, and user-centric products.''

In the Agile framework, one of the core principles is collaboration and feedback across all levels of the team. However, in Turkish cultural contexts, particularly in workplaces, individuals may be hesitant to speak openly, challenge decisions, or question the status quo, especially when it comes to those in positions of authority. This reluctance is not simply a product of hierarchical work structures but is also embedded in the very language used to communicate. The Turkish language, with its rich honorifics and formal structures, often reinforces social hierarchies, making it difficult for lower-level employees to voice concerns without fear of disrespect or negative repercussions.
For example, a product owner in a Turkish context may focus solely on the immediate results of the current sprint and may view longer-term UX improvements as secondary. In such a context, teams may feel that they have little autonomy to address root UX issues, particularly if it requires cross-functional collaboration or challenges existing hierarchies. This power distance creates a cycle of limited upward communication, where valuable user insights or design issues remain unresolved because they are not seen as important or are filtered through cultural norms that emphasize deference.

--- [Message 38] Keywords: po ---
✅ DO:

Write like a practitioner solving real problems in high-stakes environments.
Here’s how to make Medium your launchpad, not a diary:
🔥 1. Write From the Battlefield
Not from the classroom. Not from “inspiration.” From the trenches.
Ask yourself:
What have I wrestled with at Allianz, Radity, or in insurance UX?
Example topics:
“What 1 Million Insurance App Users Taught Me About Simplicity”
“Why Most Insurance Dashboards Fail—and How We Fixed It at Allianz”
“The UX Debt You Don’t See in Whitelabel Design Systems”
Make people say: “Holy sh*t. He’s been there.”
🧠 2. Be the Explainer Others Wish They Had
Break down hard problems in simple, smart language. Use visuals. Show before/after. Show what you tried, what failed, and what finally worked.
Example:
“Here’s why our initial flow failed—and how we increased purchase conversion by 14%.”
That’s catnip for PMs, UX leads, founders.
🧲 3. Reverse Engineer Your Dream Audience
Don’t write for “UX people.”
Write for the hiring manager at Amaris.
Write for the CPO in Eindhoven.
Write for the person who skims, thinks, and clicks your LinkedIn the same day.
Articles are not about followers.
They're about positioning yourself as inevitable.
🧱 4. Build a Mini-Stack of 3
Forget going viral.
Write 3 smart, specific, practical case-driven pieces in the next 45 days.
Once those are out, your name sticks.
People see your CV and go “Oh yeah—I read his post on embedded insurance UX.”
That’s what wins.
🪓 TL;DR
Writing is a weapon—but only if it cuts through noise.
Don’t write for the industry. Write to your future team.
Make each post a case study in what you do differently.
Stop caring about reach. Focus on clarity, guts, and credibility.
Write like a builder, not a blogger.
Ship. Be smart. Be specific. Be seen.

--- [Message 40] Keywords: po ---
Here is the first article

The Fragility of Agile: Cultural and Linguistic Roots of UX Disintegration in Turkish Contexts

In the rapidly evolving world of software development, Agile methodology has gained significant popularity for its promise of rapid iteration, flexibility, and close collaboration. This framework champions the idea of delivering products iteratively, enabling teams to adapt quickly to user needs and changing requirements. Yet, despite its global appeal, Agile often falters when transplanted into cultural and linguistic contexts that are misaligned with its foundational principles.

Mel Conway’s seminal insight, now widely known as Conway’s Law, holds that “organizations which design systems are constrained to produce designs which are copies of the communication structures of these organizations.” His statement, originally made in 1968, continues to resonate in today’s digital product development environments — especially where Agile and UX practices are influenced by cultural and linguistic frameworks. Nowhere is this more evident than in the Turkish context, where unique cultural and linguistic patterns — such as high power distance, collectivism, uncertainty avoidance, and high-context communication — interact with Agile’s ideals, often resulting in structural fragmentation and UX disintegration.

Conway’s cultural approach reminds us that it’s not just methodology that shapes product development, but the social architecture of the teams and organizations behind it, particularly the cultural and linguistic systems through which communication occurs. This essay explores how the Turkish language, cultural codes, and communication preferences create an environment where Agile becomes fragile, and UX becomes an afterthought — not by accident, but as an inevitable expression of deeper systemic traits rooted in language and culture.

Power Distance and Hierarchical Structures in Turkish Culture
Power distance — the degree to which less powerful members of a society defer to more powerful ones — is a fundamental cultural trait in Turkey. As defined by Geert Hofstede, Turkey ranks high on the power distance index, meaning that there is a marked gap between those in leadership positions and those at lower levels. This cultural trait deeply impacts how communication flows in Agile environments, as it creates a barrier to horizontal dialogue.

In the Agile framework, one of the core principles is collaboration and feedback across all levels of the team. However, in Turkish cultural contexts, particularly in workplaces, individuals may be hesitant to speak openly, challenge decisions, or question the status quo, especially when it comes to those in positions of authority. This reluctance is not simply a product of hierarchical work structures but is also embedded in the very language used to communicate. The Turkish language, with its rich honorifics and formal structures, often reinforces social hierarchies, making it difficult for lower-level employees to voice concerns without fear of disrespect or negative repercussions.

For example, a project may focus solely on the immediate results of the current sprint and may view longer-term UX improvements as secondary. In such a context, teams may feel that they have little autonomy to address root UX issues, particularly if it requires cross-functional collaboration or challenges existing hierarchies. This power distance creates a cycle of limited upward communication, where valuable user insights or design issues remain unresolved because they are not seen as important or are filtered through cultural norms that emphasize deference.

High-Context Communication and Fragmented Collaboration
Another significant cultural trait in Turkey is high-context communication, where much of the meaning in a conversation is implied through context, non-verbal cues, and shared understandings, rather than being explicitly stated. In Agile, where clarity and direct communication are essential to team alignment and quick iteration, high-context communication can pose significant challenges.

This preference for indirect communication is particularly evident in the Turkish language, which allows for more implicit meanings and layers of interpretation. For example, a statement like “We’ll see” or “It’s a matter of time” can often convey an entire set of assumptions or intentions without explicitly addressing the issue at hand. In Agile, where feedback needs to be clear and actionable to ensure smooth iteration, this can lead to misunderstandings or fragmented decision-making.

When teams fail to communicate explicitly, Agile’s feedback loops become shallow and ineffective. The ambiguity in communication creates space for misinterpretation, and the important decisions about design or UX are often left unspoken or glossed over. This lack of direct communication, when combined with the high-context nature of Turkish language and culture, results in fragmented collaboration where key details or concerns — such as the impact of a design flaw on user experience — are either misunderstood or ignored entirely.

Collectivism and the Reluctance for Individual Ownership
Turkey is often described as a collectivist society, where group loyalty, harmony, and consensus are prioritized over individual autonomy. This cultural framework is reinforced in the Turkish language, which places a significant emphasis on group identity and relational harmony. This collectivist mindset can often stifle individual ownership of tasks, especially in Agile environments, where personal accountability for product success is a central tenet.

In such a cultural context, the avoidance of conflict and the preference for consensus often result in UX problems being overlooked or pushed aside in favor of maintaining group harmony. In the realm of design, this reluctance to challenge the status quo can hinder the ability to iterate or improve upon the product in ways that would benefit the user. For example, a designer may recognize a critical flaw in the user interface but hesitate to bring it up during a team meeting for fear of disrupting the harmony of the group. This reluctance to take ownership is culturally ingrained, with the language itself supporting deference and collective decision-making rather than encouraging individual responsibility.

When the focus is on maintaining harmony rather than resolving underlying problems, issues related to UX are often minimized or ignored altogether. As a result, Agile teams may fail to address critical issues in the user experience, leading to disjointed, inconsistent, or ineffective designs. This preference for consensus-driven decision-making undermines Agile’s iterative approach, as it prioritizes the avoidance of conflict over the pursuit of meaningful, user-centered improvements.

The Influence of Language and Cultural Norms on Agile’s Fragility
Mel Conway’s theory of systems being shaped by organizational communication structures provides a cultural lens through which to view the challenges of Agile in Turkish contexts. If products reflect the communication systems of the cultures that create them, then the flaws in Agile processes in Turkey can be traced back to the cultural and linguistic structures that govern how people communicate and collaborate.

In many Turkish environments, Agile practices are adopted as a formality, rather than being deeply integrated into the cultural fabric of the organization. While Agile rituals such as daily stand-ups or sprint reviews may be conducted, they often remain disconnected from the true, underlying dynamics of team communication. The lack of explicit, direct communication — rooted in cultural preferences for indirectness and hierarchical deference — creates a disconnect between the intent of Agile and its execution.

For example, a project owner dismiss concerns about UX flaws with a simple statement: “We don’t have the budget or time to fix this now.” This dismissal, grounded in a cultural preference for certainty and predictability, reflects a lack of responsiveness to the ongoing needs of the product. Rather than addressing the problem, the culture of avoidance takes precedence, and the issue is left unresolved. This reluctance to challenge the status quo, coupled with the high-context communication, means that UX concerns are often deprioritized and ignored.

In this way, Agile’s promise of flexibility and responsiveness becomes weakened. The communication structure, shaped by Turkish cultural and linguistic norms, inadvertently creates a system where feedback is shallow, problems are deferred, and UX is treated as secondary. This results in a fragile Agile process, one that is ill-equipped to deliver the user-centered, iterative product development that Agile is intended to foster.

Conclusion
The cultural and linguistic dynamics within Turkish society — marked by high power distance, collectivism, uncertainty avoidance, and high-context communication — present significant challenges to the successful implementation of Agile methodology. These cultural traits, deeply embedded in the Turkish language, create barriers to clear communication, ownership, and collaboration, all of which are essential to Agile’s success.

In such a cultural context, Agile becomes a brittle framework, unable to adapt and respond effectively to the needs of users or the product. Instead of promoting iterative design and constant improvement, Agile practices become ceremonial, disconnected from the underlying communication structures that shape how teams work together. As a result, UX is often neglected, and products fail to meet the needs of users, reflecting the fragmented and incomplete communication systems that dominate Turkish cultural contexts.

Conway’s Law offers a powerful lens through which to understand this dysfunction: if the systems are shaped by the communication structures of the culture that produces them, then it is clear why UX becomes disintegrated in Turkish Agile processes. The communication structure is simply not designed to foster the open, direct, and collaborative dialogue that Agile requires. Until this cultural mismatch is addressed, Agile will remain fragile, and UX will continue to be sidelined in favor of certainty, structure, and predictability.

Thinks in systems: design systems, scalability, and consistent UI behavior.
Has writing skills for microcopy (which helps shape tone and user trust).''

--- [Message 50] Keywords: token, po ---
For the following say write me a linkedin post introduction I will share it

''Design Systems vs. Design Tokens: Clearing Up the Confusion
Murat Cetin
Murat Cetin
4 min read
·
Dec 24, 2024
1






Design systems and design tokens are two essential concepts in modern product design and development, yet they are often misunderstood and conflated. This confusion leads to misplaced assumptions and, at times, an inability to recognize the inefficiencies lurking beneath the surface. In this article, I willclarify the distinction between the two, examine why the differentiation is essential, and challenge the wishful thinking that overestimates what design tokens can achieve, especially when they’re mistakenly assumed to be a design system.

What Are Design Systems?
A design system is a comprehensive framework that includes guidelines, components, patterns, and principles to ensure consistency and scalability across a product or suite of products. It’s not just about colors, typography, or UI elements — it’s about unifying design and development efforts under a single source of truth.

Think of a design system as the “blueprint” for your design and development processes. It incorporates a shared language between teams, a governance model, and documentation that guides how to use components and patterns effectively.

Key elements of a design system:

A library of reusable components
Documentation of interaction patterns
Design principles and branding guidelines
Alignment across design and development teams
What Are Design Tokens?
Design tokens, on the other hand, are the atomic units of style — the smallest pieces of reusable design decisions that can be implemented in code. They capture design attributes like colors, spacing, typography, and shadows in a way that computers and software can interpret.

For example:

PrimaryColor: #0066FF
SpacingSmall: 8px
FontSizeHeading1: 24px
Design tokens are essentially a bridge between design and code. They provide consistency in the use of values but don’t prescribe patterns, components, or behavior.

Why People Confuse Design Systems with Design Tokens
The confusion often stems from the overlapping terminologies and their relationship. Both concepts involve creating consistency across products, but their roles are fundamentally different:

Misunderstanding Scope: People assume design tokens define behavior and patterns because they contribute to UI consistency. However, tokens address “what,” while design systems define “how” and “why.”
Tech-Led Narratives: Stakeholders view design systems narrowly, seeing them only through the lens of tokens because tokens are implemented in code. This reduces the holistic vision of design systems to a purely technical artifact.
Ambiguity in Documentation: Poorly defined systems sometimes treat design tokens as the centerpiece, leading to incomplete implementation or an overestimation of what tokens alone can achieve.
Why the Differentiation Should Be Made
Without a clear distinction between the two, teams risk inefficient workflows, unmet expectations, and wasted resources.

Design Systems Focus on Holistic Thinking: A design system captures the broader context. It considers user behavior, accessibility standards, interaction patterns, and component modularity. Tokens alone cannot ensure that your card component adapts to mobile breakpoints or provides accessible contrast ratios — it’s the design system’s documentation and guidelines that ensure these qualities.
Tokens Are Just a Tool: Tokens are helpful for centralizing and enforcing design decisions, but they don’t guide behavior. While tokens define the variables, design systems dictate the relationships and rules about how those variables interact. Assuming that a library of tokens equals a complete design system is wishful thinking.
The Wishful Thinking Trap: Design Tokens as a Shortcut
The idea that having design tokens equates to having a design system is a classic example of optimism bias. This assumption fails to consider:

The need for cross-functional alignment.
The effort required for creating guidelines, patterns, and processes.
The reality that design is about user needs, not just consistent values.
Simply adopting tokens might seem like a shortcut to a unified experience, but without a robust design system to back them, they often become isolated fragments. This approach leads to:

Teams creating inconsistent patterns.
A lack of context about how and when to use certain tokens.
Design and engineering teams misunderstanding the “why” behind decision
Making Design Systems Work with Tokens
Rather than over-relying on tokens or conflating them with design systems, teams should see the two as complementary:

Start with a Strong Foundation: Begin with design principles and a clear understanding of your product’s needs. This forms the backbone of your design system.
Leverage Tokens as Enablers: Use tokens as the variables that underpin consistent styles and ensure they integrate with your system’s governance.
Document Everything: Beyond the “what,” provide documentation for the “why” and “how” — this will serve as a guide for scalable design and development.
Conclusion
Design systems and design tokens are closely connected but serve fundamentally different purposes. A design system provides a holistic framework for product consistency, scalability, and cohesion, while design tokens serve as the small, reusable variables that make a design system practical and programmable.

Misunderstanding this distinction can lead to disappointment, unnecessary effort, and subpar results. By approaching design systems and tokens with clarity, teams can foster alignment, improve workflows, and build products that truly serve their users — not just their codebase.

UX Design
UX Research
Ux Writing
Software''

--- [Message 55] Keywords: cto, po ---
he is going to release following article on linkedin write a post

“Any organization that designs a system (defined broadly) will produce a design whose structure is a copy of the organization’s communication structure.”

Melvin Conway, 1968

In over half a century, Conway’s Law has aged gracefully — if anything, growing sharper and more revealing as digital systems, teams, and tools have proliferated. The irony? While Agile, Lean, and DevOps have transformed how we deliver software, the communication patterns and structural incentives inside many organizations remain rigid — and invisible. And nowhere is this more evident than in how UX design is silently, steadily, and systematically compromised.

Let me explain.

Backend-First Cultures: Where Structure Becomes Product
In many product teams, especially in traditional or enterprise-driven organizations, the back-end architecture isn’t just the technical foundation; It becomes the de facto product strategy.

This isn’t just a technical constraint. It’s an organizational mirror.

Teams are formed around back-end services. Responsibilities are carved along the lines of APIs, not user journeys. Prioritization is tied to database migration timelines or legacy infrastructure needs. And inevitably, the design process — ideally user-first — is reverse-engineered to fit the backend’s existing contracts, limitations, or sequencing.

The result? Users don’t experience flows. They experience fragments.

What does this look like on the ground?
Design decisions are made after the tech stack is finalized.
Flow compromises are made to fit the structure of legacy APIs.
PMs say: “This flow is too expensive to change right now — let’s go with what we have.”
Designers are asked to “just design around the back-end.”
And all of it seems logical. After all, there are real constraints: time, budget, sprint velocity, team bandwidth.

But Conway would remind us:

“If we believe our homomorphism, then we must agree that it does. To the extent that an organization is not completely flexible in its communication structure, that organization will stamp out an image of itself in every design it produces. The larger an organization is, the less flexibility it has and the more pronounced is the phenomenon.”

Budget, Sprints, and the Myth of Incremental UX
Agile has taught teams to work small, think fast, and move iteratively. But in practice, it often becomes a license to ignore system-level UX coherence.

You’ve likely heard these lines:

“Let’s just release it and see.”
“We’ll fix UX in the next sprint.”
“There’s no time to revisit the onboarding — let’s optimize the dashboard first.”
The problem isn’t urgency. It’s orientation.

Agile, as practiced in many organizations, tends to decompose product development into atomized tickets. Those tickets reflect the team’s internal structure, not the user’s mental model. When each story is implemented to match a specific service, team, or sprint commitment, UX becomes fragmented not because of incompetence, but because of structure.

Conway again:

“Given any design team organization, there is a class of design alternatives which cannot be effectively pursued by such an organization because the necessary communication paths do not exist.”

The Anatomy of Compromise
The designer sees the disjointedness. They’ve mapped the ideal flow.
The research is clear.
The friction is known.

But when they raise the issue, they hear:

“This would require rewriting the service.”
“The front-end can’t talk to that database yet.”
“We already committed the sprint.”
And so compromise is made.
Not once, but repeatedly.
And each micro-compromise calcifies into the system’s permanent shape.

Over time:

Workarounds replace holistic thinking.
UX debt accumulates.
Design systems fragment.
Users get confused — but no one remembers how it happened.
Because it didn’t happen all at once.
It happened organizationally.

So What Does Conway See?
From Mel Conway’s perspective, this isn’t a UX problem. It’s not even a dev problem.
It’s a reflection problem.

Your product isn’t broken because of code.
It’s broken because of how your teams are wired to talk — and how that shapes what gets prioritized, compromised, and eventually shipped.

This means:

If your architecture leads design, your UX will always lag.
If your structure fragments conversation, your product will fragment too.
If no one is empowered to challenge technical-first decisions, the user will always be second.
The Systems We Deserve
When organizations repeatedly compromise UX under the name of budget, sprint planning, or technical limitation, they aren’t being lazy. They’re being consistent with their structure.

The tragedy isn’t the compromise.
The tragedy is that the compromise becomes normalized.
That “designing around the backend” becomes how things are done.
That scalable, intentional, and strategic design becomes an edge case.

But again, Conway would say: that’s just how systems work. They reflect us.

And unless we change how we think, speak, and structure ourselves — we’ll keep designing systems that work beautifully for the org chart, but poorly for the human on the other side of the screen.

https://www.melconway.com/Home/Committees_Paper.html

--- [Message 76] Keywords: po ---
Most designers prep for interviews by polishing case studies and pixel-perfect screens. But when you’re in the room, the real challenge isn’t visual polish, it’s thinking clearly under ambiguity.



One book that made me sharper at this is Solving Product Design Exercises by Artiom Dashinsky.



What it teaches isn’t “how to design prettier buttons.” Instead, it pushes you to:

- Frame messy, ambiguous problems.

- Identify trade-offs and constraints.

- Prioritize ruthlessly (like a PM would).

- Communicate solutions simply and logically.



Beyond the interviews, it is a toolbox, I realized the exercises mirrored my real work in product design:



- The toughest moments weren’t about UI choices. They were about choosing the right problem to solve given tech, budget, and regulatory constraints.

- Success didn’t come from designing faster, it came from being able to explain trade-offs in a way that aligned business and tech.



That’s why this book sticks. It’s not about answers. It’s about frameworks for system-level thinking.



https://a.co/d/4As7bV3

--- [Message 84] Keywords: token, po ---
how about this version?
''Back in 2013, Paul Adams at Intercom, published one of the most timeless essays in product design: “The Dribbblisation of Design.”



He warned us that design was slowly becoming a showcase of visual polish, not system thinking — a parade of screens detached from real product behavior.



More than a decade later, the same pattern repeating.

Only now, it hides under different names: velocity, agile, MVPs, AI prototyping.



We’re still moving fast, often without building product architecture underneath.



And that’s the quiet killer of scalability.



Without architecture, without a clear logic of how flows, features, and states connect, teams end up compensating with:

- endless component updates,

- design tokens that multiply faster than use cases,

- meetings to realign what should have been aligned by design.



That’s what the 2013 article got right.



It wasn’t a critique of visuals, it was a call to return to thinking structurally, like architects, not decorators.



We need stronger product architecture, so design decisions scale, instead of scatter.



Still one of the most relevant reads today:

https://www.intercom.com/blog/the-dribbblisation-of-design/''

--- [Message 85] Keywords: po, figma ---
how about another post for the following video
https://www.youtube.com/watch?time_continue=1&v=RQSG_d0mmf0&embeds_referring_euri=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.figma.com%2F&source_ve_path=MjM4NTE

--- [Message 86] Keywords: cto, po ---
here is the transcript of the video

TRANSCRIPT
00:00
Welcome to Startup Grind. I thought what I would do for the first half hour is just talk about some things from my research that I haven’t written a lot about before, with the hope that it would be useful. Then I think you’re going to interview me and skewer me, which will be fun.
The reason why I’m looking like this is if I didn’t have a T-shirt and tie, I just wouldn’t be honored. So, that’s who I am.
This is a model of how bodies of understanding emerge—a pattern you see over and over in industry after industry. Let me describe it in the context of the organic chemical industry.
00:57
Eighty years ago, if you needed a new molecule, there were only about 50 people in the whole world who could synthesize these molecules. Dupont employed almost all of them. These people were deeply experienced—the best in the world.
When they needed to develop a new molecule, they would just reach deep and pull out all the intuition and education they could, try to synthesize something, mix a few atoms in a beaker, spin it into a fiber, and then knock on a colleague’s door: “What do you think? I just invented this.” The colleague would say, “I have no idea what you invented, but bring it in. Let’s heat it up for 10 more minutes and see what happens.”
The miracle molecules we now call nylon, polyester, acetate, Kevlar, and so on emerged from DuPont’s labs through this trial-and-error problem-solving process. Only the best in the world could play in this game.
01:44
As these artists practiced their craft over subsequent decades, patterns began to emerge. When organic chemistry moved from trial-and-error into pattern recognition, people could start teaching others how to build these molecules. There wasn’t a handbook yet, but there were patterns. You could tell your students, “If you do this, there’s a higher probability this will happen than that.”
You didn’t have to be the smartest in the world to play in this game. As a result, more people studied organic chemistry, more companies entered the fiber business, and eventually, theories like quantum theory and rate theory emerged.
03:15
As these theories, which are statements of causality, became understood, you could predict in advance that if you do this, this will be the result. You could build models to know whether a particular molecule could be built and what its properties would be. Today, if you need a new molecule, all you need is a BS in chemistry from a state school and a great piece of software. You can build better molecules faster and cheaper than the world’s best scientists of a few generations ago.
04:33
The progress from trial-and-error to pattern recognition to rules-based understanding has been a blessing to mankind. Before you go to bed at night, look around the room you’re sleeping in. You can touch at least a dozen different fibers that have been an unmitigated blessing in terms of quality, reliability, cost, performance, and durability.
These blessings didn’t come from replicating the intuition of DuPont scientists but from commoditizing their understanding through scientific progress.
05:21
The same thing is happening in medicine. Historically, and even today, if you have a problem, you go to the finest doctor you can find. They pull from their deepest experience and intuition and treat every patient as if they are different. Medicine has historically been intuitive.
As doctors practiced their craft, patterns emerged, leading to empirical or best-practices medicine. We’re not yet at rules-based medicine for many diseases, but the more we move from intuitive medicine to pattern recognition to rules-based precision medicine, the higher the quality and the lower the cost.
06:54
Venture capital, historically, has been in the realm of intuitive medicine. Many venture capitalists believe you can’t predict what will work or not, so they rely on their intuition for every potential investment. In my mind, entrepreneurship doesn’t need to be in the trial-and-error stage anymore. There is a lot that is now in the pattern recognition realm.
08:36
Some of my best friends are venture capitalists, but as a group, fewer have read my research than any other group. They believe the world is unpredictable and that they already know everything they need. I want to talk about components of patterns that increase the probability of success.
09:25
The most known piece of my work is the theory of disruption. I remember when Andy Grove gave a big talk at the Academy of Management. He organized his talk around disruption. It was lucky for me because I was coming up for tenure at Harvard. Grove told me, “You shouldn’t call it disruption; you should call it the Christensen effect.” Disruption has too many connotations, and people might misunderstand the theory. I couldn’t call it the Christensen effect, so I kept calling it disruption. Grove was right: everybody uses the word, but few understand it.
10:52
The theory of disruption allows you to predict competitive responses. It tells you whether your moves will kill incumbents or whether they will kill you. Companies, in pursuit of profit, continually improve products. If you try to win by introducing a better product for the best customers of an industry leader, they will always beat you.
12:16
Instead, if you enter at the bottom of a market with products that aren’t as good as the leader’s, but improve over time, the leader will flee upmarket rather than fight you. To kill the competitor rather than be killed, start at the bottom. All successful companies in Silicon Valley followed this pattern.
13:40
The second element relates to customers. If you have a new technology, don’t go after an established market. Compete against non-consumption.
14:27
Example: Electronics and the transistor. From the late 1940s to early 1970s, consumer electronics used vacuum tubes. They were expensive and large, so only wealthy people could afford TVs.
15:13
Transistors were a disruptive technology but couldn’t immediately replace vacuum tubes. Established companies tried to make them good enough for existing products, investing billions to overcome technical barriers.
16:47
In 1955, Sony released a $2 pocket radio. It was small, static-filled, and limited in quality, but infinitely better than nothing. Teenagers loved it because it let them listen to music privately. In 1959, Sony introduced the first portable television for $99. It was limited but accessible, delighting customers who had no other option. Over time, solid-state electronics improved, and vacuum tube companies vanished.
19:46
Lesson: Target customers whose alternative is nothing. Then, improve products iteratively.
20:26
Today, electric vehicles illustrate the same principle. Tesla’s $100k cars compete with the best vehicles in California. But the bigger opportunity is targeting parents of teenagers who need slow, short-range cars—infinitely better than nothing.
21:49
Similarly, solar electricity is not competitive in developed markets due to grid reliability. But in Mongolia, half the population has no electricity. Cheap solar products are infinitely better than nothing, creating a booming new market across Africa and Asia.
24:53
Lesson: With new technologies, compete against non-consumption first to increase your probability of success.
25:38
Third theory: Jobs to be Done. Understand what job the customer is trying to accomplish. People don’t buy based on characteristics; they hire products to get a job done.
26:27
Example: Milkshakes. A fast-food chain tried improving milkshakes using customer demographics—it didn’t work. Observation revealed morning commuters bought milkshakes for long, boring drives.
30:29
Alternatives like bananas, donuts, bagels, and Snickers didn’t work as well. Milkshakes satisfied the job: filling, long-lasting, easy to consume while driving. Competitors were other ways to accomplish the job, not other milkshakes.
32:24
The job, not the customer, is the correct unit of analysis. Designing products around the job dramatically increases success probability.
33:06
Finally, venture capital itself needs disruption. When I started my first company in 1984, Bain Capital invested in Staples. Later, as funds grew, they moved upmarket to bigger deals. Entrepreneurs at the low end struggle to get funding due to this mismatch.
36:49
That concludes my prepared remarks. Now, you can ask me questions about how venture capital and entrepreneurship might get disrupted.
[Applause] [Music]

As these theories, which are statements of causality, became understood, you could predict in advance that if you do this, this will be the result. You could build models to know whether a particular molecule could be built and what its properties would be. Today, if you need a new molecule, all you need is a BS in chemistry from a state school and a great piece of software. You can build better molecules faster and cheaper than the world’s best scientists of a few generations ago.
04:33
The progress from trial-and-error to pattern recognition to rules-based understanding has been a blessing to mankind. Before you go to bed at night, look around the room you’re sleeping in. You can touch at least a dozen different fibers that have been an unmitigated blessing in terms of quality, reliability, cost, performance, and durability.
These blessings didn’t come from replicating the intuition of DuPont scientists but from commoditizing their understanding through scientific progress.
05:21
The same thing is happening in medicine. Historically, and even today, if you have a problem, you go to the finest doctor you can find. They pull from their deepest experience and intuition and treat every patient as if they are different. Medicine has historically been intuitive.
As doctors practiced their craft, patterns emerged, leading to empirical or best-practices medicine. We’re not yet at rules-based medicine for many diseases, but the more we move from intuitive medicine to pattern recognition to rules-based precision medicine, the higher the quality and the lower the cost.
06:54
Venture capital, historically, has been in the realm of intuitive medicine. Many venture capitalists believe you can’t predict what will work or not, so they rely on their intuition for every potential investment. In my mind, entrepreneurship doesn’t need to be in the trial-and-error stage anymore. There is a lot that is now in the pattern recognition realm.
08:36
Some of my best friends are venture capitalists, but as a group, fewer have read my research than any other group. They believe the world is unpredictable and that they already know everything they need. I want to talk about components of patterns that increase the probability of success.
09:25
The most known piece of my work is the theory of disruption. I remember when Andy Grove gave a big talk at the Academy of Management. He organized his talk around disruption. It was lucky for me because I was coming up for tenure at Harvard. Grove told me, “You shouldn’t call it disruption; you should call it the Christensen effect.” Disruption has too many connotations, and people might misunderstand the theory. I couldn’t call it the Christensen effect, so I kept calling it disruption. Grove was right: everybody uses the word, but few understand it.
10:52
The theory of disruption allows you to predict competitive responses. It tells you whether your moves will kill incumbents or whether they will kill you. Companies, in pursuit of profit, continually improve products. If you try to win by introducing a better product for the best customers of an industry leader, they will always beat you.
12:16
Instead, if you enter at the bottom of a market with products that aren’t as good as the leader’s, but improve over time, the leader will flee upmarket rather than fight you. To kill the competitor rather than be killed, start at the bottom. All successful companies in Silicon Valley followed this pattern.
13:40
The second element relates to customers. If you have a new technology, don’t go after an established market. Compete against non-consumption.
14:27
Example: Electronics and the transistor. From the late 1940s to early 1970s, consumer electronics used vacuum tubes. They were expensive and large, so only wealthy people could afford TVs.
15:13
Transistors were a disruptive technology but couldn’t immediately replace vacuum tubes. Established companies tried to make them good enough for existing products, investing billions to overcome technical barriers.
16:47
In 1955, Sony released a $2 pocket radio. It was small, static-filled, and limited in quality, but infinitely better than nothing. Teenagers loved it because it let them listen to music privately. In 1959, Sony introduced the first portable television for $99. It was limited but accessible, delighting customers who had no other option. Over time, solid-state electronics improved, and vacuum tube companies vanished.
19:46
Lesson: Target customers whose alternative is nothing. Then, improve products iteratively.
20:26
Today, electric vehicles illustrate the same principle. Tesla’s $100k cars compete with the best vehicles in California. But the bigger opportunity is targeting parents of teenagers who need slow, short-range cars—infinitely better than nothing.
21:49
Similarly, solar electricity is not competitive in developed markets due to grid reliability. But in Mongolia, half the population has no electricity. Cheap solar products are infinitely better than nothing, creating a booming new market across Africa and Asia.
24:53
Lesson: With new technologies, compete against non-consumption first to increase your probability of success.
25:38
Third theory: Jobs to be Done. Understand what job the customer is trying to accomplish. People don’t buy based on characteristics; they hire products to get a job done.
26:27
Example: Milkshakes. A fast-food chain tried improving milkshakes using customer demographics—it didn’t work. Observation revealed morning commuters bought milkshakes for long, boring drives.
30:29
Alternatives like bananas, donuts, bagels, and Snickers didn’t work as well. Milkshakes satisfied the job: filling, long-lasting, easy to consume while driving. Competitors were other ways to accomplish the job, not other milkshakes.
32:24
The job, not the customer, is the correct unit of analysis. Designing products around the job dramatically increases success probability.
33:06
Finally, venture capital itself needs disruption. When I started my first company in 1984, Bain Capital invested in Staples. Later, as funds grew, they moved upmarket to bigger deals. Entrepreneurs at the low end struggle to get funding due to this mismatch.
36:49
That concludes my prepared remarks. Now, you can ask me questions about how venture capital and entrepreneurship might get disrupted.
[Applause] [Music]

This is a model of how bodies of understanding emerge—a pattern you see over and over in industry after industry. Let me describe it in the context of the organic chemical industry.
00:57
Eighty years ago, if you needed a new molecule, there were only about 50 people in the whole world who could synthesize these molecules. Dupont employed almost all of them. These people were deeply experienced—the best in the world.
When they needed to develop a new molecule, they would just reach deep and pull out all the intuition and education they could, try to synthesize something, mix a few atoms in a beaker, spin it into a fiber, and then knock on a colleague’s door: “What do you think? I just invented this.” The colleague would say, “I have no idea what you invented, but bring it in. Let’s heat it up for 10 more minutes and see what happens.”
The miracle molecules we now call nylon, polyester, acetate, Kevlar, and so on emerged from DuPont’s labs through this trial-and-error problem-solving process. Only the best in the world could play in this game.
01:44

When organic chemistry moved from trial-and-error into pattern recognition, people could start teaching others how to build these molecules. There wasn’t a handbook yet, but there were patterns. You could tell your students, “If you do this, there’s a higher probability this will happen than that.”
You didn’t have to be the smartest in the world to play in this game. As a result, more people studied organic chemistry, more companies entered the fiber business, and eventually, theories like quantum theory and rate theory emerged.
03:15

--- [Message 92] Keywords: po ---
Here is another article third in a row to share

How to do a Product Critique
Watch and learn

Julie Zhuo
Julie Zhuo

Follow
6 min read
·
Jun 17, 2014
9.8K

22


All right, you caught me. The title of this article is a bit too fancy-pants. Product Critique is some dressed-up corporate lingo. It brings to mind whiteboards scrawled over with strong-smelling blue marker depicting arrows connecting abstract concepts like product-market fit and value proposition. It oozes I am an interview question, and while admittedly, it is my favorite thing to chat with candidates about, the language turns the whole thing into something much more formal than it ought to be.
Which is—in its simplest form—exploring the curiosity of why some products and experiences work for people, and why others don’t.
Listen, product design isn’t some innate skill one is born with, like having a good ear for distinguishing notes or possessing exceptionally powerful twitch muscles for sprinting across an Olympic stage. Developing good product intuition—by which I mean developing a good sixth sense about what features or experiences will resonate with people and become successful—is about two core tenets: 1) understanding people’s desires, and 2) understanding how people react to things.
Thankfully, all of us are people (except for those of us on the Internet who are dogs), and all of us react to things. So if you’re looking around for how to starting honing your product intuition, start with yourself and how you personally experience anything new.
As an example, let’s say you’re looking to try out a new app. You download it from the store and fire it up, ready to begin your product critique.
Hold up—before the loading screen even blinks into view for the first time, there’s a lot to consider:
How did this app come to your attention? Was it word of mouth from a friend? Interesting. Why’d she tell you about it? Or did you read about this app in an article? If so, what compelled you to actually go find it and download it? Was it recommended? Did it have a catchy icon and an intriguing name? Had you heard it about it before, and if so, how many times before? Why didn’t you download it then, and what eventually tipped you over towards trying it out now?
What’s your one-line summary of what this app does at this stage? It’s interesting to compare the answer to this question before using the app and after.
What’s the buzz so far? Do you have a sense of whether it’s popular? Whether it’s useful? Slick? Next-level? Did you happen to glance at the ratings, reviews, and description as you were downloading the app?
Thinking about the ways in which your first impressions of a new app are formed helps you better understand an app’s value proposition and marketing, and what the team behind it is doing—either implicitly or explicitly—to create that kind of impression.
Get Julie Zhuo’s stories in your inbox
Join Medium for free to get updates from this writer.

Subscribe
Now it’s time to open the app and play with it. Spend as much time as you would normally (which is probably more like a few minutes rather than half an hour), and ask yourself the following:
What’s the experience of getting started or signing up? Was it easy, with just a few button taps, or was it challenging with a bunch of verification steps?
How does this app explain itself in the first minute? Was it clear about what it did and how it works? Did the messaging feel friendly? Confusing? Did you read and retain the information, or did you skip it because it felt long-winded and boring?
How easy to use was the app? Did you understand immediately what the app was about, or did you need to tap around to try things and figure it out? Are there a lot of navigational items and buttons and gizmos to process, or did the entire thing feel familiar, contained and simple?
How did you feel while exploring the app? Did a cute illustration, funny quip, or piece of really interesting content make you smile? Did the navigation frustrate you because you couldn’t figure out how to get back to a previous screen? Did you feel smarter and more efficient for using this app? Did you take note of a particular detail and think Wow, I’ve never seen that before?
Did the app deliver on your expectations? Looking back to what you thought the app would do, did it match your experience of actually using it? If it’s a content app, was the content engaging? If it’s a utility app, did it solve the identified problem in a meaningful manner?
How long did you spend using the app? Time generally correlates with interest. If you spent a long time on the app, what about it kept you using it?
Just like with people, most opinions of a product are formed within the first few minutes. A quick run-through gives you a sense of whether the app actually provides value, is easy to use, and feels well-crafted.
After your first use of an app, the days and weeks following are just as important to consider broader questions of stickiness and growth:
How often have you used the app? When do you tend to use it? What compels you to open it? Is it because of enticing push notifications? Or because all your friends are still talking about and using it? Is it because you’ve found yourself relying on it again and again? Has this app transcended all obstacles to achieve the esteemed goal of becoming a regular habit in your life? Why or why not?
How does this app compare to other similar apps? Which things does it do better or worse? What would make you choose to use this app versus something similar?
What do other people think of this app? Getting additional perspectives is one of the quickest ways to learn about what works and what doesn’t in the broader market. Read reviews. Read comments in blogs. Read tweets about this app. Ask your friends what they think. Ask your cousins who live in a different town from you. Does their impression match yours? In what ways is it different, and why do you think it’s different?
Based on all that you know, how successful do you think the app will be a year from now? Take a real stance. Write it down in a digital sticky note somewhere. It can be private. You don’t have to shout it out to the world. But capturing a clear and honest bet so that you can go back and look to see if you were right or wrong is one of the most objective ways to combat hindsight bias.
(And eventually, after enough time has passed), were you right in your prediction of how this app was going to do? If not, why not? How were your own personal preferences and tastes similar or different from people in the broader market? You must understand this in order to better calibrate your opinions in the future.
There is no shortcut to developing better product instincts besides keen and close observation. By going through the above questions for everything you experience—using a new app, booking travel, standing in line at Disneyland, returning a package—you start to pick up on what details lead to what reactions.
Great experiences doesn’t just happen by happenstance. The best designers and product thinkers know people. They understand what motivates and what delights and what intrigues. They have strong theories about why any successful product or service is successful, and why any failed product or service has failed. They know this because they have been watching and studying people—including themselves—for a long, long time.
This process doesn’t have to be labeled a product critique. It doesn’t have to be a test or an interview question. It doesn’t have to happen because of any other person or obligatory cause.
It just has to happen because you’re curious, over and over and over again. Because you want to learn how to build good things.

Post

Post

Share

Save

Print
After ten years as an entrepreneur, I started writing a blog called Startup Lessons Learned. My original goal was to provide support and encouragement to other entrepreneurs who, like me, had some unorthodox ideas about how to run companies. Along the way, I encountered two big surprises. They led me to a question I had never previously considered: is it possible that entrepreneurship is actually a management science?

For most of us, the phrase “management science” conjures up a decidedly non-entrepreneurial image, and for good reason. The preeminent management science, for much of the twentieth century was general management, pioneered by twentieth century giants like Peter Drucker and Alfred P. Sloan. As an entrepreneur, I often saw the best practices of general management fail startups. Applied out of context, they were dangerous.

This conflict has played out in many companies that I’ve worked with. My most recent startup created a marketplace for customers to buy and sell virtual goods for their 3D avatar. So you can imagine how I expected some skepticism when pitching ideas about technology innovation to, say, the U.S. Army. This was my first surprise: they understood that innovation needs to be understood at the level of principles, not just tactics.

Some tactics, like Continuous Deployment, are controversial, even among people with my background. But the really vexing questions came from entrepreneurs who inundated me with questions from other backgrounds. Could these techniques be used by enterprise software companies? Hardware manufacturers? Biotech startups?

Answering those questions requires understanding the principles of entrepreneurship in a rigorous way. I began to call this new methodology “the lean startup.” It is a rigorous application of lean principles to the problem of new product innovation.

Inspired by the lean manufacturing revolution (and excellent books like Lean Thinking), I started with a first fundamental question: in a startup, what activities are value-creating and which are waste? Usually, new projects are measured and held accountable to milestones and deadlines. When a project is on track, on time, and on budget, our intuition is that it is being well managed. This intuition is dead wrong.

Most startups fail because they are building something that nobody wants. Enamored with a new technology or a radical new product, many entrepreneurs never find a set of customers who will buy it. Each new feature added to such a product is actually wasted effort, even if it’s done on-time and on-budget. In product that nobody wants, all the features get thrown away.

This problem vexes venture-backed founders and general managers alike. Anyone who is tasked with creating disruptive innovation will find it familiar. In short, for an innovation on the wrong course, executing well doesn’t increase the odds of success. What’s needed, I realized, is a new definition of progress in a startup, one that recognizes that a startup’s primary mission is to penetrate the fog of the unknown and find out what their customers ultimately will want and accept. I call this unit of progress “validated learning about customers.” Because learning is the goal, the progress that startups make requires a very different kind of management.

This led me to a second surprise. As I was traveling the country talking to people about the lean startup, I kept meeting people in the audience who seemed out of place. They weren’t what we usually think of as entrepreneurs. They were general managers, most working in very large companies, who were tasked with creating new product innovations. They were smart, well-read, and understood Clay Christenson’s Innovator’s Solution cold.

They were adept at organizational politics: they knew how to form autonomous divisions with separate P&Ls, and could shield controversial or disruptive teams from meddling. And — my biggest surprise yet — they were visionaries. Like the startup founders I have worked with for years, they could see the future of their industries, and were prepared to take bold risks to seek out new and innovative solutions to the problems their companies would face.

They found the lean startup helpful. In fact, they considered it obvious that these principles — rapid iteration, a focus on validated learning, and reducing cycle time from idea to learning — applied to their situation. Here’s why. In my quest to put the practice of entrepreneurship on a rigorous footing, I had begun with this definition of a startup:

A startup is a human institution designed to create a new product or service under conditions of extreme uncertainty.




Originally, I used this definition to help entrepreneurs understand why the “best practices” of general management so often failed them. They can’t thrive when transplanted into the soil of extreme uncertainty that is the province of startups. But this definition is surprising: it doesn’t say anything about the size of the company, sector of the economy, or industry. Looking past my old preconceptions, I realized that entrepreneurs are everywhere: in government, in the occasional garage, and yes, in the traditional organization.

All entrepreneurs face the same fundamental challenges:

How do we know if we’re making progress?
How do we know if customers will want the product we are building?
And, if they do, how do we know what kind of value we can create with it?
But because every startup also strives to become an institution, answering these questions requires more than just disciplined thinking at the whiteboard. It requires the coordination of many different people, working in concert to answer them. In other words, it requires management.

The management challenges presented by entrepreneurship are different. For example, how do we hold an entrepreneurial team accountable for making progress? This is a core challenge faced by venture capitalists and corporate CFOs alike. A team might be making progress against their plan, might be hitting milestones, might even be earning revenue. But none of those artifacts constitute progress. Learning is progress. In short, it’s demonstrating the ability to use what we’ve learned to change customer behavior, with key metrics as the common source of evidence.

To measure learning requires a shift of attention from the output of models to their inputs. I’ll show you how to apply this to entrepreneurial situations in my next post.''
